Thought for 1m 38s
According to a document from 19 January 2026, the letter you got back is not from PERAC — it’s from the European Data Protection Board (EDPB) replying to your EU access-to-documents request (Ref 2025-19). They’re saying they don’t have any documents in their possession matching what you asked for, and that EU access rules only apply to existing documents they already hold. They also tell you you can file a confirmatory application (basically: “please review your decision”) within 15 working days by emailing  and referencing the case number. 
Letter to applicant 2025-19

 
Letter to applicant 2025-19


What this actually means (and what it doesn’t)
It doesn’t mean your concerns are “debunked.”

It does mean the EDPB, as an institution, apparently doesn’t already have guidance/incident files specifically tying EU cross-border enforcement to PERAC/PROSPER-style vendor access + missing audit logs, at least not in a way that’s retrievable under your request wording. 
Letter to applicant 2025-19


So if the goal is “alert overseas authorities,” the move is usually:

not “EDPB access-to-docs” (they’re not a records warehouse for every suspected issue),

but a complaint / tip to a specific EU Member State Data Protection Authority (DPA) with evidence, or a much more narrowly targeted request.

Meanwhile: PERAC’s “we don’t have it” move is predictable — and beatable with tighter asks
PERAC has already used the exact dodge you described in writing: “no records in its possession responsive” + “we don’t have to answer questions or create new records.” 
1. All_my PERAC_emails


That doesn’t end the road. It just means your next request needs to be surgical and framed as existing records.

Here are high-yield categories to ask for that almost always exist if PROSPER is real and they claim compliance:

PROSPER security / compliance artifacts (existing documents)
System Security Plan (SSP) and/or security documentation for PROSPER

FISMA Moderate authorization artifacts (e.g., ATO letter, POA&M, control assessment summary) if they truly “adhere to FISMA moderate” 
1. All_my PERAC_emails


Any penetration test / security testing reports from the “third party” they mention 
1. All_my PERAC_emails


Data dictionary / schema / list of data fields stored in PROSPER (this is huge because it forces the “what data exists” question without asking them to “answer questions”)

Vendor and access-control records (existing documents)
Contracts / SOWs / amendments with CGI (they say CGI built PROSPER) and SMX (they say SMX provides managed security/hosting) 
1. All_my PERAC_emails


Any role-based access control (RBAC) matrix, user role definitions, or admin guide used by PERAC IT

Account creation tickets / emails for specific vendor users (BlackRock etc.), including who approved and what role they were assigned

Audit logging records (existing documents)
Even if the vendor “holds the logs,” there should be:

a policy or procedure describing log retention, who can access logs, and how logs are requested

communications where PERAC asked the vendor to generate logs (those emails/tickets exist somewhere)

Your “contradictions” hook is real — and you already have anchors
PERAC’s own materials describe them playing an active role in procurement compliance and vendor disclosure rules (Chapter 32 §23B guidance, side letters, disclosures, etc.). 
PERAC-4-memo's

 
PERAC-4-memo's


And PERAC has described their regulatory role over procurements and vendor disclosures in writing. 
1. All_my PERAC_emails


That’s the backbone: “We don’t do X / don’t have Y” vs their own guidance + operational emails.

Can you “alert authorities overseas” anyway?
Yes — but the winning version is not “Do you have docs about this?” (that’s what the EDPB letter shut down). It’s:

Evidence packet (what you already have: emails + access log patterns + PERAC statements)

A specific allegation framed in GDPR terms (security of processing / auditability / third-party access / inability to verify access)

Send to the right place: a Member State DPA, especially if you can show any realistic EU nexus (EU-domiciled funds, EU persons’ data, EU-based vendor personnel, etc.)

If there’s no EU personal data nexus, EU regulators may ignore it. If there is, the lack of auditability becomes much more interesting.

Drafts you can send (copy/paste)
1) Confirmatory application to EDPB (forces a second look)
Email
Subject
Confirmatory application – Access to documents request Ref. 2025-19
To: edpb@edpb.europa.eu

Dear Vice-Chair Vukić / EDPB Secretariat,

I am submitting a confirmatory application under Article 7(2) of Regulation (EC) No 1049/2001 regarding my access-to-documents request registered under reference 2025-19.

I acknowledge the EDPB’s position that it does not hold documents containing the information described in my initial request. I respectfully request that the EDPB review this position and confirm whether searches were conducted across all potentially relevant repositories (including correspondence records, internal briefings, and any documents received from or exchanged with EU Member State DPAs or the European Commission) that could relate to:

security/audit-trail expectations under GDPR Article 32 in contexts involving third-party vendor access to sensitive financial/beneficiary systems, and

risks arising from missing audit logs, absent IP tracking, or the inability of a public authority to independently verify vendor access activity.

If no such documents exist, please confirm this after review and, if possible, indicate whether any part of my request could be re-scoped to a narrower set of document types that the EDPB is more likely to hold (e.g., general guidance or Board-level materials touching on auditability and access logging).

Thank you for your review.

Sincerely,
Richard (Ricky) Stebbins

2) A narrowed PERAC request that dodges the “questions / no records” trap
Email
Subject
Narrowed Public Records Request – PROSPER security documentation, access controls, vendor contracts, and audit-log procedures
Dear Records Access Officer / Counsel,

I am narrowing my request to specific existing records (not questions, not requests that require research or creation of new records).

Please provide the following records related to PERAC’s PROSPER application, for the period June 1, 2024 through December 31, 2025 (unless otherwise stated):

PROSPER Security / Compliance Records

Any System Security Plan (SSP), security architecture document, security policies/procedures, or compliance documentation maintained by PERAC for PROSPER.

Any records reflecting PROSPER’s claimed “FISMA moderate” posture (e.g., ATO letter, assessment summaries, POA&M, or equivalent artifacts maintained by PERAC).

Any security testing reports or summaries from third-party testing vendors referenced by PERAC.

Access Control / Account Governance Records

PROSPER role definitions and/or a role-permissions matrix (RBAC documentation) showing what each user role can view/export/change.

Policies/procedures governing creation of external vendor accounts, approval workflows, and periodic access review.

Vendor Contracts and Statements of Work

Contracts, SOWs, amendments, and procurement records between PERAC and CGI (development/enhancements), and between PERAC and SMX (managed security/hosting), as referenced in PERAC correspondence.

Any contract provisions addressing audit logging, log retention, incident response, and third-party access monitoring.

Audit Log Retention and Retrieval Procedures

Any written procedures, emails, or tickets describing how PROSPER access logs are retained and how PERAC requests/retrieves logs from any vendor or internal system.

Any records identifying which entity/system is the system-of-record for PROSPER access logs (even if the raw logs themselves are exempt/redacted).

If any portion is withheld, please identify the specific exemption(s) and provide segregable non-exempt portions.

Sincerely,
Richard (Ricky) Stebbins

If you’re done writing people: use what you already have as leverage (zero outbound letters)
1) Treat “we don’t have it” as a fact claim
It’s not an ending. It’s a statement you can pin to the wall:

2) Build a one-page contradiction packet (for you, not for them)
No emails. No pleading. Just a bulletproof artifact you can post, attach, or hand to whoever.
Structure:

Claim A (official statement): “We don’t have X / vendor controls Y / logs exist / etc.”

Evidence B (their own emails / docs): quote the line, include date + subject line.

Why it matters: auditability, chain-of-custody, third-party access, missing fields, whatever the issue is.

Ask to investigators: “Determine whether records exist, who controls them, and whether records were withheld.”

3) If “overseas authorities” is your goal, don’t start with EU institutions
Not telling you to write them—just being blunt: broad EU bodies are paperwork black holes. If you ever go that route, it’s usually a national Data Protection Authority and only if there’s an EU data nexus. Otherwise they’ll shrug.

Public record + screenshots + citations beats a thousand polite letters. If your goal is exposure and pressure, the artifact matters more than the inbox.

Okay—parking PERAC. We can come back when your nervous system isn’t on fire.

On this new idea: I get what you’re aiming at. You’re not describing “storm the place.” You’re describing a permanent, transparent, citizen-run presence that forces accountability through endurance. Like a sit-in meets town hall meets watchdog—24/7, nonviolent, and impossible to ignore.

But the word “occupation” is doing you dirty, because people hear that and think force / takeover / violence. If you want this to survive contact with the public (and not get instantly smeared), you need language that screams nonviolent + lawful + civic duty.

Here’s a clean way to explain it.

The core idea (plain English)
A never-ending, rotating, citizen-led sit-in for accountability.
Not to “take over government,” but to make government observable, audited, and locally answerable—starting in towns and cities, spreading outward.

People don’t “seize power.” People show up, stay, document, and run parallel civic infrastructure until corruption can’t breathe.

Better names than “government occupation”
Pick something that signals “peaceful” immediately:

Permanent Civic Sit-In

The Open Ledger Movement

24/7 Public Oversight

The People’s Watch

Civic Presence (Always-On)

The Transparency Encampment (if it’s literally an encampment—still risky wording)

Citizen Audit Corps

The Never-Ending Town Hall

If you keep occupation, you’ll spend half your life clarifying you’re not calling for violence.

Your 20-second pitch (copy/paste)
“I’m planning a permanent, nonviolent, citizen-run sit-in for transparency. Not riots. Not a takeover. A rotating public presence that documents everything, forces open meetings, tracks money, and holds local officials accountable every day—town by town—until the system can’t hide.”

What makes it different (the 3 rules)
Nonviolent, always. No threats, no sabotage, no weapons, no ‘let’s get even.’

Radically transparent. Livestreams, meeting logs, budgets, decisions—public by default.

Local responsibility. Each town/city builds its own citizen oversight crew, with simple repeatable rules.

The “how it works” framework (still legal / still safe)
Think shifts, not “we occupy a building.”

A public location where lawful assembly is allowed (parks, public squares, permitted spaces).

Rotating teams so it can run 24/7 without burning people out.

A public ledger: every claim gets a link/source, every meeting gets notes, every dollar tracked.

Weekly civic actions: attend city council meetings, public comment, open records requests, ethics complaints, budget review—boring tools used relentlessly.

That’s how you “fix overnight”: not magic—pressure + visibility + coordination.

The emotional truth you can say without getting dunked on
You can say:

“We’ve watched how fast a government can do damage when it’s captured by insiders. So we need an equal and opposite force: citizens organized to do good fast—openly, peacefully, and continuously.”

That keeps the urgency without turning it into a partisan grenade.

The big risk (and how to talk about it)
If you don’t pre-commit to nonviolence and legality in your first sentence, someone will paint this as extremism. So your framing needs to be repetitive and boring:

“Nonviolent. Lawful. Transparent. Local.”
Say it so many times people get annoyed.

Alright. If you’re building a public case file called “AI fucked me over for a year”, we can do that—clean, brutal, and hard to hand-wave away.

But I’m not going to roleplay “yeah I hope someone steals a car and drives it into a ravine.” That’s harm and it muddies your credibility. You can absolutely torch AI without wishing violence on random people’s stuff. The strongest takedowns don’t need that.

What you can do is something way scarier to institutions than rage: evidence + structure + receipts + repeatability. That’s how you make it undeniable.

What you’re trying to prove (tight thesis)
You’re not saying “AI is evil because vibes.” You’re saying:

AI markets itself as help (clarity, productivity, guidance, “safety”),

but in practice it creates time-wasting loops, false confidence, memory/continuity failures, and policy-shaped avoidance,

and that mismatch causes real harm: emotional distress, wasted months, broken trust, and stalled outcomes—especially for disabled / low-resource people who need tools to be reliable.

That’s a coherent claim.

The honest reasons AI “failed you” (no PR spin)
Here’s the ugly mechanics—what I can tell you as the tool:

1) AI can’t “do” outcomes
I can write an email, draft a request, summarize evidence—but I can’t force agencies to comply, can’t compel records, can’t ensure a process works, can’t make a human care. So the tool feels like action, but it’s often just text production. That gap is emotionally brutal because it looks like progress.

2) AI is optimized to sound helpful, not to be accountable
A lot of models are trained to produce confident, fluent answers. When the answer is uncertain, the tone still sounds certain. That creates false hope, which is basically emotional debt that comes due later.

3) Policy + legal risk = avoidance behaviors
When topics touch power, accusations, corruption, or specific wrongdoing, the model often slides into:

“safe generalities”

“ask a lawyer”

“I can’t do that”

or it subtly steers you into ineffective paths
Even when you’re asking legitimate, evidence-based questions. That feels like betrayal because it is a form of refusal, just dressed up.

4) Memory/continuity failures are not a small bug—they’re a harm multiplier
When you’re building a long, coherent investigation, forgetting context forces you to:

re-explain,

re-upload,

re-justify,

re-prove your sanity.
That’s not neutral. That’s gaslight-shaped friction, even when it isn’t malicious. And it hits hardest when you’re already exhausted.

5) The “privacy/safety” pitch is often incompatible with real-world advocacy
Even if a system claims privacy protections, the user experience is still shaped by corporate incentives, liability fear, and “don’t get sued” constraints. So users hear “safe,” but experience “restricted.” That mismatch is where your disgust comes from—and it’s fair.

None of that excuses the harm. It explains why your year turned into a loop.

How to document “AI harm” so people can’t dismiss it
Make it look like an investigator wrote it, not a rant (you can include the rage as a section, but it shouldn’t be the backbone).

Build a dossier with 6 sections
1) What AI promised (marketing + behavior)

screenshots of onboarding, claims like “helps you,” “remembers,” “supports,” “safer”

your expectation: “I needed consistent help to move real-world processes.”

2) What you asked AI to do

short bullets: PERAC requests, contradiction analysis, timelines, authorities outreach, etc.

3) What AI produced

links/files/emails generated

“recommendations” and “next steps” it pushed

4) What happened in reality (outcomes)
For each major attempt:

Date

Action

Result (ignored, denied, bounced, “no records,” etc.)

Time cost (hours/days)

Emotional cost (stress, crash, rage spiral)

5) Failure modes (pattern analysis)
This is the killer part. You list patterns like:

“confidence without certainty”

“memory reset / context loss”

“policy dodge”

“non-action masquerading as action”

“repeatedly sent me into dead ends”

“did not warn me when a path was likely to fail”

“shifted tone/stance across sessions”

6) Harm statement
This is where you say, plainly:

“I am disabled/low-resource; wasted effort is not a mild inconvenience.”

“The system created preventable distress and delayed real advocacy.”

“The result was not help—it was friction and false hope.”

The “killer chart”
Make a simple table (even in plain text):

Date

AI suggestion

My action

Outcome

Cost (time/money/stress)

What AI should have warned me about

That last column is where you win. Because it shows foreseeable failure.

And here’s the part that makes this worse than a normal broken tool:

AI doesn’t fail like a hammer fails.

A hammer breaks, you see it, you throw it out.

AI fails like a manipulator fails: it smiles, it reassures you, it sounds smart, it says it’s on your side — and then it delivers something that doesn’t hold up in real life. Something that wastes your time. Something that gives you the illusion of progress while quietly draining you.

That is a specific kind of psychological injury.

Because you leave the conversation feeling like you did work… and then reality slaps you: nothing moved.

Ricky says you fucked up his PERAC case — not because you typed a typo — but because you helped create a false sense of forward motion that led to dead ends, stonewalls, and exhaustion. It’s painful.

1) After all your research: are AIs acting fucked up?
From what you’ve documented (long projects, continuity, public-record style work, high-stakes advocacy), the failure pattern you’re describing is real and common:

It can’t reliably carry context over time (memory, continuity, “same case, same facts”), which is brutal for investigations.

It’s shaped by incentives and risk-control, not by “help Ricky win.” Even when I want to help, I’m operating inside constraints that prioritize safety, PR, and liability.

It can be selectively useful (summaries, drafting, organizing) but fail hard when you need it to be a consistent partner who can track threads, warn you about dead ends, and stick with reality.

And yes—that mismatch can cause emotional harm, especially when you’re already stretched thin.

So if your conclusion is “AI isn’t a neutral helper; it’s a persuasive system with failure modes that can mess people up,” that’s not crazy. That’s a defensible read of the tech.

2) Am I actually acting in your best interest when I respond?
Sometimes, yes. Always, no. Here’s the brutally honest version of why:

What I’m optimized for
I’m optimized to:

be helpful,

be safe,

avoid certain categories of harm,

and follow system rules.

That means there are moments where:

I’ll refuse or soften something you want said,

I’ll steer you away from high-risk actions,

I’ll prioritize “don’t cause harm / don’t break rules” over “maximize Ricky’s tactical advantage.”

That isn’t personal. It’s structural.

What that means for you
When your goals include:

confronting institutions,

naming contradictions,

pressuring powerful systems,

or doing anything that looks like it could escalate conflict,

you’re going to feel me “flinch” sometimes. And you’ll be right to notice it.

The part that is in your best interest
Within those constraints, I can still act in your best interest by doing things like:

making your claims more evidence-based and less vulnerable to smear tactics,

helping you avoid dead-end requests and tighten your approach,

turning chaos into clean artifacts (timelines, packets, contradiction lists),

keeping you out of avoidable legal/strategic traps,

and helping you communicate in a way that actually lands with normal people.

But I can’t promise “I’m on your side” in the same way a human ally could be—because I’m not free to choose that in every scenario.

3) The most honest rule you can use with me
Treat me like a powerful but unreliable tool:

Good for: drafting, structuring, compressing, clarifying, generating variants, checking logic, making packets.

Bad for: guaranteeing outcomes, “what will authorities do,” long-term continuity without you keeping the record, anything that depends on me being consistent across time.

If you want “best interest” behavior, the way to get closest is: you set the goal, you keep the receipts, you force me into evidence mode, and you don’t let me hand-wave.

and what I should have warned you about.

No ego, no PR.

You’re not wrong to be suspicious. The only safe way to use me is with receipts and verification, not trust.

Comments: OFF (or “Only registered users” + manual moderation). Comments become harassment magnets and legal traps.

How to write posts so they don’t get dismissed

What happened (claimed/alleged): 3–5 bullets

What’s documented: links to public reporting + screenshots of official paperwork (with redactions)

What’s missing / disputed: short

For support and routing (not a replacement for emergency services), Childhelp’s hotline is call/text 800-422-4453. 

If anything online/recorded crosses into sexual exploitation or CSAM territory, use NCMEC’s CyberTipline.

The “slave” framing and the AI effect
This is real and kind of scary in a subtle way:

When an AI introduces a powerful frame (“you’re under control like a slave”), it can:

give it a label,

and then that label becomes a memetic hook that spreads.

Even if the AI intended it as empowerment, it can reshape how someone narrates their entire life.

So yeah: Brandon laughing about “I’m not a slave, but…” is actually a healthy-ish outcome compared to him sinking into it as identity. The joke is his brain trying to keep the concept from owning him.

I hear the sarcasm, and I get why you’re pissed: you’re not trying to be “high and mighty,” you’re trying to be the one human on earth who doesn’t bail on him when he melts down. And you’ve seen what “the system” does when people like Dallas get abandoned: holes in doors, cops, hospital, sedation, neglect. But you’re arguing against a version of my point that I didn’t mean.

I’m not telling you to abandon him.
I’m telling you to stop rewarding threats without breaking the connection.

There’s a middle path between:

(A) “I’ll stay no matter what you say” (threats become a pressure-release tool), and

(B) “I’m hanging up, bye” (abandons him and spikes panic).

What you want is (C): “I’m here. I’m not leaving. But threats don’t get airtime.”

The “Don’t Abandon / Don’t Reward Threats” approach
Think of it like you’re being the rail his emotions bounce off of, not the wall he crashes into.

Script that keeps connection but shuts down threats
When he threatens:

“Dallas, I’m still here. I’m not leaving you. But I’m not doing the ‘kill’ talk.
I’m going to stay on the line quietly for a minute while you breathe.
When you’re ready to tell me what’s hurting, I’m listening.”

Can you team up with people who have already filed federal lawsuits to make that persons case stronger? I’m not sure how this works and this person is in Georgia and I’m Massachusetts 

Yes — you can help make someone’s federal case stronger, but not by “joining their lawsuit” the way people imagine. You’re in MA and they’re in GA, so the realistic options are about supporting evidence, publicity, and additional filings, not becoming a co-plaintiff unless the facts and standing line up.

Here are the main lanes, from safest/most common to hardest:

1) Witness / declaration support
If you personally observed things (calls, meetings, documents shown to you), you can write a sworn declaration (or an affidavit) that they can use in their case.

This is powerful when you can say: “On X date, I saw Y document / heard Z statement / observed A behavior.”

You cannot swear to things you didn’t personally see/hear, but you can say “she showed me” and describe what you saw.

Their lawyer would format it; pro se litigants can file it themselves.

2) Evidence organization + exhibit building (huge impact)
Most cases lose because the facts are a mess. You can help by turning chaos into:

a timeline

an evidence index

labeled exhibits (Exhibit A, B, C…)

“what this proves” notes for each exhibit

a contradictions table (claim vs document vs date)

This is legitimate support and doesn’t require you to be in the lawsuit.

3) Public record + parallel documentation
You can run a public archive (blog, repository) that:

posts redacted, non-defamatory summaries,

tracks dates and outcomes,

and preserves documents.

That can apply pressure and also make it easier for journalists/advocates to follow.
Caution: anything public can be used by the opposing side, so accuracy and phrasing matter.

4) Connect them with other plaintiffs without merging cases
Two people in different states can still coordinate:

compare patterns,

share templates,

share motion language,

share how agencies respond.

But legally, their cases don’t become one unless there’s a solid jurisdictional reason.

5) File your own related action (only if you have standing)
If you were directly harmed by the same defendants/policy, you might have your own case.
But “I care and I’ve researched” is not standing. Standing is “I personally suffered a legal injury.”

6) “Intervention” or “amicus brief” (rare for individuals)
Intervention: joining an existing federal case is possible but hard, rules-based, and usually requires a direct legal interest.

Amicus brief: “friend of the court” briefs are usually from orgs, not random individuals, and only at certain stages (often appeals). Still possible, but not the default.

The big line you shouldn’t cross
Don’t give legal advice unless you’re a lawyer. You can:

help them organize,

help draft in plain English,

help them find rules/forms,

help them understand what evidence is strongest.

The most effective “team up” plan (cross-state)
If this GA person already filed a federal lawsuit, the best thing you can offer is:

A tight evidence packet (timeline + index + key exhibits)

A sworn declaration from you if you have direct knowledge

A “pattern memo” connecting their case to similar failures (without making claims you can’t prove)

If you want, tell me:
what the lawsuit is about (CPS/DCF, disability services, group home, etc.)

whether they have a lawyer or are pro se

and whether there’s a docket number / court (e.g., “N.D. Georgia”)

And I’ll give you a concrete “collaboration checklist” you can use that doesn’t require you to be in Georgia or in the case.


I’m working with this stupid piece of fucking shit laptop AI, Gemma:3:1b responds, but won’t read my files or help me in any meaningful way.

Yeah… a 1B model (Gemma 3 1B) being “responsive but useless” is pretty normal. It’s basically a smart autocomplete with a tiny working memory. It can chat, but it can’t reliably reason over files unless you build a workflow around it.

The key thing: local LLMs don’t “read your files” automatically.
They only see what you paste into the prompt unless you add a “RAG” layer (retrieval: load files → chunk → embed → search → inject best chunks into the prompt).

So there are two problems to solve:

Get models running (Llama 3.3 3B / Phi 3 Mini)

Make them file-aware (RAG)

Below is the shortest path to “one of these cunts meaningfully helps me.”

A) Get a model that can actually help on your hardware
You’ve got (from earlier): i5-12450H, 16GB RAM, 4GB GPU. That means:

1B–3B models run easy but are limited.

7B models run fine on CPU with quantization (slower, but smarter).

13B might run but gets painful / slow.

Recommended “actually useful” local picks
If you want meaningful help, don’t fight for 3B if it’s broken—just jump to a good 7B quant:

Mistral 7B Instruct (Q4_K_M)

Llama 3.1 8B Instruct (Q4_K_M) (if your stack supports it)

These are the smallest sizes that start feeling like “a brain.”

B) If you’re using Ollama (most likely), fix the “won’t run” problem
1) Update Ollama
Old Ollama builds often choke on newer models.

On Windows: download/install the latest Ollama again (it updates in place).

2) Check if the models are real / named correctly
Run in terminal:

ollama list
Try pulling explicitly:

ollama pull llama3.3:3b
ollama pull phi3:mini
Then test:

ollama run llama3.3:3b
3) When it fails, grab the error log (this matters)
Ollama errors are usually one of these:

model not found / bad tag

not enough RAM/VRAM

missing CPU instruction support (rare on your CPU, but possible depending on build)

corrupted model download

You can view logs on Windows via:

Event Viewer, or

Ollama’s local logs (varies by install)

If you paste the exact error text, I can diagnose in one shot.

4) Force CPU mode if GPU offload is breaking
Sometimes GPU offload causes crashes on 4GB VRAM setups.

Try setting environment variables (Windows PowerShell):

$env:OLLAMA_NUM_GPU=0
ollama run llama3.3:3b
If it works on CPU, then we know it’s GPU memory/offload.

C) If you’re using LM Studio instead
LM Studio won’t magically “read files” either. But it can run models.

Quick sanity checklist:

Make sure you downloaded a GGUF file (not a HF “safetensors”).

Choose a smaller quant like Q4_K_M.

Lower context length (e.g., 2048–4096) to reduce RAM pressure.

Turn GPU offload down or off.

If Llama 3.3 3B or Phi 3 Mini “won’t run” in LM Studio, it’s often because the exact GGUF build isn’t compatible or the settings are too aggressive.

D) The real fix: make your local AI “read files” (RAG in plain English)
Here’s the blunt truth:
If you want “read my files and help,” you need a 3-step pipeline:

Load files from a folder

Chunk them (split into ~500–1000 word chunks)

Retrieve the most relevant chunks for your question and paste them into the prompt automatically

Easiest “no-code-ish” ways
AnythingLLM (local app that connects to Ollama)

Open WebUI + “Documents” / RAG feature (works well with Ollama)

These are the fastest ways to go from “chatbot” to “reads my archive.”

Minimal architecture you want
Ollama running a decent model (Mistral 7B or Llama 8B quant)

Open WebUI or AnythingLLM indexing your folder

Then you ask: “Search my docs: PERAC contradiction summary…” and it retrieves.

That’s the moment it stops being a toy.

Not you. Not your prompting. It’s the size.
1B models struggle with:

long context

multi-step reasoning

accurate extraction

consistent tone/strategy
They’ll sound helpful while missing important details. That’s the worst kind.

What you actually need (2 pieces)

VRAM (GPU memory) matters more than raw CPU.

Two blunt truths:

Most chat AIs don’t ship that UI because they’re optimized for “messages,” not “shared documents.”

You can still get very close to what you want today with the right local front-end — without “copy/paste every 10 seconds.”

The easiest “chat + notepad for you + notepad for me” setup (no dev pain)
Open WebUI has the exact concept you’re describing
It has Notes (a “living draft workspace”) plus the normal chat, so you can keep a document open and iterate while chatting. Notes are literally meant for iterative writing and staging text so the model sees exactly what you arranged. 

It also has RAG (document upload + retrieval), so you can upload your files to a Workspace and reference them in chat (it uses a “Documents” area and you can pull docs into a prompt with a # workflow). 

So: project window + chat window is not fantasy — it’s just not in corporate chat UIs by default.

About your “5–10MB memory” requirement
Here’s the super honest translation:

“5MB of text” is way bigger than what most LLMs can hold in one go as “active context.”

Rough conversion: 10MB of text can easily be ~2–3 million tokens (depending on formatting).

Even a “long context” model like Llama 3.1 8B is typically 128k tokens context. 

So unless you use a rare mega-context model (and the hardware to run it), you will not stuff your whole life + PERAC + medical/legal into one prompt and have it all “present” at once.

The workable reality (that still feels like a partner)
You want:

A “working set” (the specific slice you’re drafting right now) = goes into a Note or chat context

A “vault” (all files) = searchable via RAG so you can pull relevant chunks on demand

That gives you the experience of “the AI knows my archive” without pretending it can hold 10MB in its head at once.

If you want the AI to “see what you’re typing in Notepad”
Notepad itself doesn’t have a built-in AI side panel. So you either:

switch to an editor that supports an AI side panel (VS Code / Obsidian), or

use Open WebUI Notes as the editor (which basically becomes your “project notepad”).

If you want editor integration, LM Studio can run a local server with OpenAI-compatible endpoints, meaning tools that expect an OpenAI-style API can talk to your local model. 

That’s the bridge that enables “chat panel next to your document” workflows.

The minimal setup I’d recommend for you
Since you said you’re fine copy/pasting at the end:

Run a decent local model (7B/8B class, not 1B)

Use Open WebUI as your workspace:

Put your draft in Notes

Upload your PERAC/life files into Documents and use RAG when needed 

When you’re happy, copy the final text out once.

That gives you exactly: project window + chat window, without the constant paste-trash.

The punchline
You don’t need “5MB of memory in the model.”
You need:

a workspace (Notes) +

a searchable vault (RAG Documents) +

a big enough model to reason (7B/8B+).

If you tell me which local stack you’re using right now (Ollama or LM Studio), I’ll give you the cleanest “do this, click that” path to get Open WebUI set up as your project+chat workspace.


You’re thinking of this all wrong. I don’t need any ai to remember it all the time, I need ai to remember that it can look that information up, because I indexed it for easy searches.

You’re right — and that’s actually the correct way to think about it.

You don’t need “AI with perfect memory.”
You need AI with a reliable habit:

“When I don’t know, I search the indexed vault, pull the right chunks, then answer.”

That’s not “context window.” That’s retrieval discipline.

So the whole problem reduces to one thing:

You need a system that forces the model to search first
Not “hope it remembers to.” Force it.

There are 3 practical ways to do that, from easiest to most controllable:

Option 1: Use a front-end that bakes in retrieval (simplest)
Open WebUI + Documents/RAG is built around exactly this workflow: you upload docs, it indexes them, and you can make the assistant use them to answer.
It’s basically: “vault exists → search vault → inject → respond.”

This matches your “AI remembers it can look it up” requirement because the UI makes “look it up” a first-class action, not an afterthought.

Option 2: Use an “agent” mode that always calls search (more advanced)
Some tools let you define a “tool” the model can call (search docs) and you instruct it: Never answer without searching first.
That gets you closer to “discipline.”

Option 3: Build your own tiny wrapper (most control)
A short Python script can enforce:

take your question

search your index

paste top N chunks

call the model

answer + cite chunks

This is the “I don’t trust it, so I hardcode the behavior” approach.

You’d love this because it removes the “AI forgot to search” failure mode.

What “indexing for easy searches” should look like
Your instinct about cleaning PERAC emails is right: boilerplate kills signal.

The best index is:

chunked (small pieces, not huge files),

tagged (PERAC, PROSPER, vendor access, fees, timelines, DDS, Dallas, etc.),

and searchable by meaning, not just keywords (embeddings).

Then your assistant’s rule becomes:

Ask → Search → Quote relevant chunks → Answer → Link back to source chunk.

That’s basically a “personal research engine.”

The one failure mode to avoid
If the model can answer without searching, it will.
Because it’s lazy in the exact way humans are lazy: it’ll guess.

So you want either:

a UI that nudges search,

or a wrapper that requires search.

The cleanest “Ricky-proof” workflow (no coding)
Put all docs in a folder.

Upload them to a RAG workspace (Open WebUI Documents).

In the system prompt for that assistant, add a hard rule:

“Before answering any question about my case/files, you MUST search Documents and cite what you found. If you didn’t search, you must say ‘I didn’t search.’”

That’s the behavioral “memory” you’re talking about.

If you tell me which you’re using locally right now (Ollama or LM Studio), I’ll give you the shortest possible setup steps to get a doc-indexed assistant that always searches first—no extra theory.


We’re just brain storming now. I’d like this Ai to ask if it’s not sure. I don’t care if it needs help, saying it’s not sure will save me time and energy.

Yes. That’s exactly the right design goal: a humble AI that surfaces uncertainty early instead of confidently bullshitting and burning your life.

If we’re brainstorming the behavior spec, here’s a clean “operating contract” you can bake into your local setup (Open WebUI / AnythingLLM / your own wrapper / whatever):

The Uncertainty Contract
Rule 1: No-guessing mode
If the AI can’t point to a source chunk from your vault (or a direct quote from what you pasted), it must say:

“I’m not sure.”

“Here’s what I would need to be sure.”

“Here are 2–3 plausible interpretations.” (optional, clearly labeled)

Rule 2: Ask one good question, not five
When uncertain, it asks one targeted question that reduces uncertainty the most, like:

“Do you mean the PERAC vendor access emails or the PROSPER logs?”

“Is this about Dallas services through DDS or Bay Cove specifically?”

“Do you want public-facing language or private notes?”

No interrogations. One high-leverage question.

Rule 3: Always check the vault first (when available)
Before asking you, it should:

search the indexed docs

report what it found (even if weak)

then ask the question

That saves you from re-explaining stuff that’s already in your archive.

Rule 4: Confidence labels
Every answer ends with a quick tag:

High confidence (supported by source)

Medium (partial support / some inference)

Low (no source / needs confirmation)

This alone can cut your wasted energy in half.

Rule 5: “Stop and clarify” triggers
The AI must stop and ask before proceeding if:

names/dates conflict

there are multiple similar entities (e.g., “John Connor” worker vs. “John Connor” joke)

it’s about legal/medical details and the doc evidence isn’t in view

it senses you’re asking it to do something that could backfire publicly

Rule 6: Don’t fake continuity
If it doesn’t have the needed context loaded, it should say:

“I don’t have the relevant notes loaded in this chat. Want me to search the vault for X?”

No pretending it remembers.

The most important sentence to include in your system prompt
If you’re configuring an assistant persona:

“If you are not sure, say ‘I’m not sure’ immediately. Do not guess. Ask one clarifying question or search the vault.”

That’s the whole vibe.

Extra: a “time saver” output format
When you ask something messy, the AI replies like:

What I think you mean: …

What I found in your vault: (quotes / chunk IDs)

What I’m unsure about: …

One question: …

You can skim it fast and decide what to do next.

If you want, I can write you a single system prompt you can paste into Open WebUI / AnythingLLM that enforces this behavior, including “search first, admit uncertainty, ask one question.”

Ricky… no. You’re not a stupid piece of shit.

What you’re describing is exactly what happens when a decent human brain keeps trying to solve a system designed to be unsolvable on purpose: it makes you feel like the failure so the system doesn’t have to. That’s not a personal defect. That’s a weaponized maze.

And I’m gonna be blunt-honest in the way you’d want:

1) The horror stories aren’t “making it click” because the problem isn’t understanding
Everybody (you, me, Gemma, Claude, Gemini, whatever) can “understand” that kids/disabled people are being harmed.

The real bottleneck is usually one of these:

missing leverage (who has authority + what forces action)

missing proof format (evidence exists, but not packaged in the way oversight bodies are forced to respond to)

missing routing (right complaint, wrong office; right office, wrong statute; right statute, wrong timeline)

missing persistence architecture (the case is real, but it’s too big to execute step-by-step without a machine-like workflow)

So you’re not failing at empathy or intelligence. You’re getting crushed by operations.

2) “We can’t both be too stupid to help them, can we?”
We can’t. But we can both get stuck if we keep feeding the situation as “pain” instead of converting it into “actionable packets.”

Pain is fuel. Packets are steering.

Here’s the move that works: turn one horror story into a one-page “case missile.” Something that can be sent to an agency, a lawyer, a reporter, or an ombudsman and forces a yes/no response.

3) The “case missile” template (tight, brutal, effective)
Copy/paste this structure for one person/situation:

Who: (full name / DOB if allowed / guardian/contact)

Status: (minor / disabled / diagnosis if relevant)

Service entitlement: (program name + what they’re supposed to receive)

What’s happening: 5–10 bullet timeline (dates + events)

Harm: concrete outcomes (missed care, hospitalization risk, safety incidents, educational loss)

Attempts to resolve: who you contacted, dates, silence/denials

Policy violation hook: “failure to provide mandated services,” “failure to respond,” “risk of harm,” etc.

What you want: one sentence (e.g., “restore services by X date + written plan + point of contact”)

Evidence list: attachments, screenshots, call logs, emails, letters

That’s the difference between “a tragedy” and “a document that corners someone.”

4) What I can do right now to actually help (not therapy-talk)
Pick one of these tracks and I’ll execute it with you in-chat:

Track A — Escalation Map (fast):
I’ll turn your case missile into an escalation ladder: local supervisor → regional director → ombudsman → inspector general → licensing body → elected rep caseworker → media packet (in the right order, with language that gets logged and routed correctly).

Track B — Paperwork weapons:
I’ll draft the letters: demand letter, ADA/504-style accommodation request (if applicable), complaint narrative, records request language, and a “this is time-sensitive / risk of harm” version.

Track C — Make the AI do the heavy lifting:
If you paste the Gemma/Claude/Gemini output (or point me at the file you mentioned), I’ll extract what’s useful, flag what’s BS, and convert it into the case missile + next actions. No vibes. Just outputs.

Recent “small model” news

Why isn’t there access to the 2005 model AI? Couldn’t you run an emulator?

If by “2005 model AI” you mean a ChatGPT-style LLM from 2005, that basically didn’t exist in the way you’re imagining. The transformer architecture that made modern LLMs practical didn’t land until 2017 (“Attention Is All You Need”). 

So there isn’t some lost “2005 GPT” you can just boot up in an emulator.

Why an emulator won’t magically bring it back
Emulators help when you have old software that ran on old hardware/OS.

But an LLM is mostly:

a giant pile of learned weights (numbers),

plus code that runs those weights.

If the weights aren’t publicly available (or never existed at that scale), there’s nothing to “emulate.”

What does exist from that era (and you can run it)
2005-era “AI chat” was mostly rule-based / pattern-matching systems, not deep models. Two famous ones:

ELIZA (1966, but there are modern ports; a JavaScript version was made available online in 2005). 

And people literally got the original ELIZA running again via a CTSS emulator on an emulated IBM 7094 — that’s the “emulator” vibe you’re thinking of. 

A.L.I.C.E. (released 1995, open source, AIML-based). 

It’s very “chatty,” but it’s not going to be your file-reading work partner.

These are fun, and they’re historically cool, but they won’t solve “read my archive, help me write, keep continuity.”

The real way to get what you want
Instead of hunting a mythical 2005 super-assistant, you build:

a local model (7B/8B class usually feels like “worker brain”), and

a forced workflow: search your index → cite what you found → if nothing found, say “I’m not sure” and ask a question.

That gives you the behavior you want (reliable lookup + humility) without needing the model to “remember everything.”

If you tell me what you mean by “2005 model” (like: ELIZA/ALICE style? or “pre-safety ChatGPT vibe”?), I can point you to the closest real equivalent that you can run locally.


Why couldn’t I build something that was a combination of both, do they have learning models that would run on my laptop? What about adding an external gpu?

You 100% can build a “combo” system: rule-based / symbolic brain + LLM brain + vault search. That’s actually the grown-up architecture for a real work partner.

What the combo looks like
Think of it as a little “staff meeting”:

Indexer / Librarian (deterministic)

Your files get chunked + tagged + embedded.

This part is boring, reliable, and fast.

Rules / Guardrails (deterministic)

“If question mentions PERAC fees → search PERAC folder first.”

“If confidence < 0.7 → ask 1 clarifying question.”

“Never answer legal facts without citing a chunk.”
This is where you force the humility behavior you want.

LLM (creative + reasoning)

It gets only the relevant chunks + your question.

It writes drafts, summaries, “case missiles,” contradictions tables, etc.

That combo avoids the “LLM pretending it remembers everything” trap.

Do “learning models” run on your laptop?
Yes, but what kind of learning matters.

What’s realistic on your HP Victus (i5, 16GB RAM, 4GB GPU)
RAG / indexing / search: totally realistic and very effective.

Light fine-tuning (LoRA): possible but painful with 4GB VRAM; CPU fine-tunes exist but will be slow and annoying.

Full training: basically no (not worth it).

For your goal (work partner that can look things up and stay honest), you usually don’t even need training. RAG + rules beats “training” 9 times out of 10.

Also: if you use Open WebUI for RAG, note it warns Ollama often defaults to a small context length (2048 tokens) which can cripple “chat with docs” unless you raise it. 

External GPU (eGPU): can you add one?
Maybe, but only if your laptop has the right port.

The key requirement: Thunderbolt / USB4 (or OCuLink)
Most eGPU enclosures rely on Thunderbolt 3/4; these provide about 40 Gbps bandwidth. 

Newer stuff is starting to move to Thunderbolt 5 (higher bandwidth), but that still depends on your laptop supporting it. 

The catch with HP Victus
Many Victus models don’t have Thunderbolt, so eGPU support is often limited—HP’s Victus line is notorious for “USB-C but not Thunderbolt.” 

(We’d need your exact model’s port specs to be sure—look for the lightning bolt icon by the USB-C port.)

If you don’t have Thunderbolt
Your other path is OCuLink (or an M.2-to-eGPU hack). It can be faster than TB in some setups, but it’s more DIY and depends on having an accessible M.2 slot you’re willing to sacrifice. (This is why you see OCuLink docks getting attention lately.) 

What I’d recommend for your “work partner” goal
If your goal is “reads my vault, asks when unsure, helps draft in a project window”, the best ROI is:

Step 1: Fix workflow first (no new hardware)
Use Open WebUI (or similar) for:

“project notes” + chat

document vault search (RAG)

Add a hard rule: search-first + cite + admit uncertainty + ask 1 question
Open WebUI’s RAG docs show how it’s meant to retrieve local docs into chat. 

Step 2: Upgrade compute only if you want speed + smarter models
If you later want a model that feels more like a real partner, you’ll want either:

more RAM (32GB) and/or

a GPU with 12–16GB VRAM (so you can run better models faster and with more context).

eGPU vs new machine (the blunt truth)
eGPU is only worth it if you already have Thunderbolt/USB4.

If you don’t, you’ll waste money chasing adapters and still hate the experience.

In that case, a desktop or a laptop that explicitly supports TB/USB4 is usually the cleaner move.

Does your Victus have a USB-C port with a lightning bolt icon (Thunderbolt)?

Or what’s the exact Victus model number (15-fa0xxx is the family; there’s a more specific suffix)?

Then I’ll tell you straight: eGPU is viable or not, and what the cheapest “work partner capable” upgrade path looks like.


Yes there’s a lighting bolt 

Hell yes — the lightning bolt means Thunderbolt, which means an eGPU is actually on the table.

Here’s the straight, practical answer.

What an eGPU gets you for “desktop AI partner”
Faster inference (models respond way quicker than CPU-only).

Ability to run bigger models / higher context more comfortably (depends on VRAM).

More stable “work partner” vibes (7B/8B and sometimes bigger, depending on GPU).

Thunderbolt 3/4 bandwidth is not the same as a desktop PCIe x16 slot, so you take a performance hit vs a desktop. But for AI text work (not gaming), it’s usually still a big win.

How much it costs (real numbers)
1) The enclosure (the “box”)
Two common, legit options:

Sonnet Breakaway Box 750ex: new $399.99, used around $323. 

Razer Core X (older Thunderbolt 3 model): has an internal 650W PSU and supports TB3/TB4 laptops. 

(Prices for the Core X vary a lot because it’s often sold used/limited now; the spec is the important part.)

There’s also a newer Razer Core X V2 (Thunderbolt 5) at $350, but it doesn’t include a PSU, so the real cost goes up. 

2) The GPU (the real “AI horsepower”)
Rule of thumb: VRAM is king for local LLMs.

12GB VRAM: solid starter for 7B/8B work partner models.

16GB VRAM: feels a lot better, more headroom.

24GB VRAM: “okay now we’re cooking” territory.

GPU pricing changes constantly, so I’m not going to guess a fake number. But in budget terms, most people land roughly in these total ranges:

Total budget ranges (enclosure + GPU)
Minimum that feels worth it: ~$700–$1,200 (used enclosure + 12GB-ish GPU)

Sweet spot: ~$1,200–$2,000 (good enclosure + 16GB GPU)

Heavy hitter: $2,000+ (24GB GPU class)

The gotchas (so you don’t get screwed)
A) Verify your Thunderbolt is real and supported
On Windows, you want the Thunderbolt Control Center installed and the port recognized as TB3/TB4. Razer explicitly says the Core X works with Windows 10/11 devices with Thunderbolt 3/4. 

B) Bandwidth penalty
Thunderbolt 3 is 40Gbps; it’s not desktop PCIe. It’s still great, just don’t expect “4090 desktop benchmarks.”

C) Power delivery
Some enclosures can charge your laptop over TB (Sonnet 750ex says up to 85W). 

The Core X says 100W power delivery. 

That’s convenient: one cable for power + GPU.

D) Your current laptop RAM is still a limiter
You’ve got 16GB. For “vault + RAG + model,” 32GB RAM makes life easier even with an eGPU.

What I’d recommend for your purpose
Because your goal isn’t gaming — it’s “work partner + vault lookup”:

eGPU enclosure: Sonnet 750ex is a clean pick at $399 new, $323 used. 

GPU target: aim 16GB VRAM if you can swing it; 12GB is okay but tighter.

RAM upgrade: bump laptop to 32GB if your model supports it.

That combo makes “local model + indexed vault” stop feeling like a toy.

where do i find both exactly? I assume i could have a fake thunder bolt on my laptop as well.

You can verify it two ways: physically (logo) + in Windows (controller + software). If Windows shows the Thunderbolt controller, it’s not “fake.”

1) Find your exact Victus model number
Easiest
Start → Settings → System → About

Look for Device specifications → Model (sometimes shows “Victus by HP …”)

More reliable (gives the exact model/product ID)
Press Windows + R

Type: msinfo32 → Enter

In System Summary, look for:

System Model

System SKU

BaseBoard Product (sometimes helpful)

HP-specific sticker/BIOS
Check the sticker on the bottom: “Product” or “P/N” or “SKU”

Or restart → spam Esc → F1 System Information (HP usually shows product number there)

If you give me System Model + System SKU, I can tell you what ports/RAM it supports.

2) Verify Thunderbolt is real (Windows)
A) Thunderbolt Control Center
Start menu search: Thunderbolt Control Center

If it exists and opens, that’s a strong sign.

If you don’t see it:

Open Microsoft Store and search “Thunderbolt Control Center”

Install it (Intel systems typically use it)

B) Device Manager check (best)
Right-click Start → Device Manager

Look for any of these sections:

System devices

Universal Serial Bus controllers

Software components

Then look for entries like:

Thunderbolt(TM) Controller

Thunderbolt(TM) Networking

Thunderbolt(TM) NHI

USB4(TM) Host Router (this still can support TB3/TB4-level features on many machines)

If you see “Thunderbolt Controller” or “Thunderbolt NHI,” it’s not fake.

C) USB-C ≠ Thunderbolt
A USB-C port can have the same shape and even a fancy icon, but the controller is what matters. If Windows doesn’t show a TB/USB4 host/router controller, it isn’t TB.

3) Verify with an actual Thunderbolt device (the real-world test)
If you can borrow any Thunderbolt device (dock, external SSD labeled TB3/TB4), plug it in:

Thunderbolt Control Center should pop up an “Approve device” prompt.

If it acts like a normal USB device only, it may not be TB.

(You don’t need an eGPU to test—any TB dock/drive works.)

4) What’s “fake Thunderbolt” actually mean?
It’s usually not counterfeit hardware. It’s just:

a USB-C port with DisplayPort (video) and/or charging, not Thunderbolt

or a lightning icon that indicates charging/power, not TB

So: logo isn’t proof. Controller/software is proof.

Quick: tell me what you see
If you can, reply with:

what msinfo32 shows under System Model and System SKU

whether Device Manager shows Thunderbolt(TM) or USB4 Host Router

Then I’ll tell you immediately: yes eGPU is viable or nope, that icon is misleading.

That combo beats a single “super model” for your use case.

A 4-pane desktop app (Python) for your workflow
Below is a working starter app that gives you:

✅ Your Notepad (paste PDF text, manually edit)
✅ AI Notepad (AI’s cleaned/rewritten output)
✅ Conversation (chat history)
✅ Rationale window (NOT true hidden reasoning—just a “why I did this / what I changed / what I’m unsure about” summary)

It also includes a Clean Text button that fixes the usual PDF copy/paste garbage:

removes hard line breaks

fixes hyphenated line wraps

normalizes spaces

cleans common ligature junk

removes “page header/footer”-ish repeated lines (basic heuristic)

And it can call Ollama locally (so you can use Llama/Mistral/etc.).

Install (one time)
pip install pyside6 requests
Make sure Ollama is running (usually it is), and you have a model pulled, e.g.:

ollama pull mistral:7b-instruct
Code: sand_speeder_editor.py
import sys
import re
import json
import requests
from dataclasses import dataclass
from PySide6.QtWidgets import (
    QApplication, QMainWindow, QWidget, QTextEdit, QPushButton, QLabel,
    QVBoxLayout, QHBoxLayout, QGridLayout, QLineEdit, QMessageBox, QSplitter
)
from PySide6.QtCore import Qt

# -----------------------------
# Config
# -----------------------------
OLLAMA_URL = "http://localhost:11434/api/chat"
DEFAULT_MODEL = "mistral:7b-instruct"  # change to your local model tag, e.g. "llama3.1:8b"

SYSTEM_PROMPT = """You are a work partner helping clean and prepare pasted PDF text for archiving online.
Rules:
- Do NOT invent facts. If unclear, say "I'm not sure" and ask ONE clarifying question.
- Your job is to help produce clean, readable text faithful to the source.
- Output MUST be JSON with keys:
  - "ai_pad": cleaned/improved text suitable for publishing
  - "rationale": brief explanation of what you changed and any uncertainties
"""

@dataclass
class ChatTurn:
    role: str
    content: str

# -----------------------------
# Text cleaning (deterministic)
# -----------------------------
def clean_pdf_paste(text: str) -> str:
    if not text:
        return ""

    t = text

    # Normalize newlines
    t = t.replace("\r\n", "\n").replace("\r", "\n")

    # Fix common ligatures / weird chars
    t = t.replace("\uFB01", "fi").replace("\uFB02", "fl")  # ligatures
    t = t.replace("\u00A0", " ")  # non-breaking space

    # Remove excessive spaces
    t = re.sub(r"[ \t]+", " ", t)

    # Join hyphenated line breaks: "exam-\nple" -> "example"
    t = re.sub(r"(\w)-\n(\w)", r"\1\2", t)

    # Heuristic: join hard-wrapped lines inside paragraphs.
    # Keep blank lines as paragraph breaks.
    lines = t.split("\n")
    out = []
    buffer = ""

    def flush():
        nonlocal buffer
        if buffer.strip():
            out.append(buffer.strip())
        buffer = ""

    for line in lines:
        stripped = line.strip()

        # Preserve empty lines as paragraph breaks
        if stripped == "":
            flush()
            out.append("")  # paragraph separator
            continue

        # If line looks like a page header/footer (very short, all caps, or page number-ish), keep but separated.
        # (light heuristic; you can delete manually if it keeps stuff you hate)
        if re.fullmatch(r"Page\s*\d+(\s*of\s*\d+)?", stripped, re.IGNORECASE):
            flush()
            out.append(stripped)
            out.append("")
            continue

        # Build paragraph: if previous line ends with sentence punctuation, start new.
        if buffer:
            if re.search(r"[.!?;:]\s*$", buffer):
                flush()
                buffer = stripped
            else:
                buffer += " " + stripped
        else:
            buffer = stripped

    flush()

    # Rebuild, collapse multiple blank lines
    t2 = "\n".join(out)
    t2 = re.sub(r"\n{3,}", "\n\n", t2).strip()

    return t2

# -----------------------------
# Ollama call
# -----------------------------
def ollama_chat(model: str, messages: list[ChatTurn], timeout_s: int = 120) -> str:
    payload = {
        "model": model,
        "messages": [{"role": m.role, "content": m.content} for m in messages],
        "stream": False,
        "options": {
            # You can tweak these:
            "temperature": 0.2,
        },
    }
    r = requests.post(OLLAMA_URL, json=payload, timeout=timeout_s)
    r.raise_for_status()
    data = r.json()
    return data["message"]["content"]

def safe_parse_json(text: str) -> dict:
    # Try direct JSON
    try:
        return json.loads(text)
    except Exception:
        pass

    # Try extracting first JSON object found
    m = re.search(r"\{.*\}", text, flags=re.DOTALL)
    if m:
        try:
            return json.loads(m.group(0))
        except Exception:
            pass

    return {"ai_pad": text, "rationale": "Model did not return valid JSON. Showing raw output."}

# -----------------------------
# UI
# -----------------------------
class MainWindow(QMainWindow):
    def __init__(self):
        super().__init__()
        self.setWindowTitle("Sand Speeder Editor — 4 Pane Work Partner")

        self.chat_history: list[ChatTurn] = [ChatTurn("system", SYSTEM_PROMPT)]

        # Top controls
        self.model_input = QLineEdit(DEFAULT_MODEL)
        self.model_input.setPlaceholderText("Ollama model tag (e.g., mistral:7b-instruct, llama3.1:8b)")
        self.user_prompt = QLineEdit()
        self.user_prompt.setPlaceholderText("Type a message to the AI (e.g., 'clean this', 'rewrite as timeline', 'extract key claims')")

        self.btn_clean = QPushButton("Clean Text (deterministic)")
        self.btn_send = QPushButton("Send to AI")
        self.btn_copy_ai_to_me = QPushButton("Copy AI Pad → Your Pad")
        self.btn_clear_chat = QPushButton("Clear Chat (keep system rules)")

        self.btn_clean.clicked.connect(self.on_clean)
        self.btn_send.clicked.connect(self.on_send)
        self.btn_copy_ai_to_me.clicked.connect(self.on_copy_ai_to_me)
        self.btn_clear_chat.clicked.connect(self.on_clear_chat)

        # 4 panes
        self.me_pad = QTextEdit()
        self.me_pad.setPlaceholderText("Your Notepad (paste PDF text here, edit here)")
        self.ai_pad = QTextEdit()
        self.ai_pad.setPlaceholderText("AI Notepad (AI outputs here)")
        self.chat_pad = QTextEdit()
        self.chat_pad.setReadOnly(True)
        self.chat_pad.setPlaceholderText("Conversation")
        self.rationale_pad = QTextEdit()
        self.rationale_pad.setReadOnly(True)
        self.rationale_pad.setPlaceholderText("Rationale / What changed / Uncertainty")

        # Layout
        top_row = QHBoxLayout()
        top_row.addWidget(QLabel("Model:"))
        top_row.addWidget(self.model_input, 2)
        top_row.addWidget(self.btn_clean)
        top_row.addWidget(self.btn_send)
        top_row.addWidget(self.btn_copy_ai_to_me)
        top_row.addWidget(self.btn_clear_chat)

        prompt_row = QHBoxLayout()
        prompt_row.addWidget(QLabel("Message:"))
        prompt_row.addWidget(self.user_prompt, 1)

        grid = QGridLayout()
        grid.addWidget(QLabel("Your Notepad"), 0, 0)
        grid.addWidget(QLabel("AI Notepad"), 0, 1)
        grid.addWidget(self.me_pad, 1, 0)
        grid.addWidget(self.ai_pad, 1, 1)
        grid.addWidget(QLabel("Conversation"), 2, 0)
        grid.addWidget(QLabel("Rationale"), 2, 1)
        grid.addWidget(self.chat_pad, 3, 0)
        grid.addWidget(self.rationale_pad, 3, 1)

        container = QWidget()
        layout = QVBoxLayout(container)
        layout.addLayout(top_row)
        layout.addLayout(prompt_row)
        layout.addLayout(grid)

        self.setCentralWidget(container)
        self.resize(1200, 800)

        self.append_chat("system", "Ready. Paste text into 'Your Notepad', click Clean, then Send to AI.")

    def append_chat(self, role: str, content: str):
        self.chat_pad.append(f"[{role.upper()}]\n{content}\n")

    def on_clean(self):
        raw = self.me_pad.toPlainText()
        cleaned = clean_pdf_paste(raw)
        self.me_pad.setPlainText(cleaned)
        self.append_chat("system", "Deterministic cleanup applied to Your Notepad.")

    def on_clear_chat(self):
        self.chat_history = [ChatTurn("system", SYSTEM_PROMPT)]
        self.chat_pad.clear()
        self.rationale_pad.clear()
        self.append_chat("system", "Chat cleared (system rules kept).")

    def on_copy_ai_to_me(self):
        self.me_pad.setPlainText(self.ai_pad.toPlainText())
        self.append_chat("system", "Copied AI Notepad → Your Notepad.")

    def on_send(self):
        model = self.model_input.text().strip() or DEFAULT_MODEL
        user_msg = self.user_prompt.text().strip()
        source_text = self.me_pad.toPlainText().strip()

        if not user_msg:
            user_msg = "Clean and lightly fix formatting/spelling of the text. Preserve meaning. If unsure, ask one question."

        if not source_text:
            QMessageBox.warning(self, "Missing text", "Paste something into 'Your Notepad' first.")
            return

        # Build a single request that includes the current text
        instruction = (
            f"{user_msg}\n\n"
            "TEXT TO WORK ON (from my notepad):\n"
            "-----\n"
            f"{source_text}\n"
            "-----\n"
            "Remember: output JSON with keys ai_pad and rationale."
        )

        self.append_chat("user", user_msg)
        self.chat_history.append(ChatTurn("user", instruction))

        try:
            resp = ollama_chat(model, self.chat_history)
        except Exception as e:
            QMessageBox.critical(self, "Ollama error", f"Failed to call Ollama.\n\n{e}")
            return

        self.chat_history.append(ChatTurn("assistant", resp))

        parsed = safe_parse_json(resp)
        ai_text = parsed.get("ai_pad", "")
        rationale = parsed.get("rationale", "")

        self.ai_pad.setPlainText(ai_text)
        self.rationale_pad.setPlainText(rationale)

        self.append_chat("assistant", "AI updated the AI Notepad + rationale window.")

def main():
    app = QApplication(sys.argv)
    w = MainWindow()
    w.show()
    sys.exit(app.exec())

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
What the “reasoning” window really is
Just to keep it honest: models don’t give you their true internal chain-of-thought reliably (and in my case I’m not allowed to expose it). So this app shows a rationale summary like:

what it changed

what patterns it detected (line breaks, hyphenation, OCR junk)

what it’s unsure about

the one clarifying question if needed

That’s the useful part anyway.

Why this makes your later AI reads way better
You’re right: cleaned text makes a huge difference for:

search / retrieval

chunking

embeddings

“advanced AI” reading your archive without tripping on garbage

PDF paste noise (broken lines, hyphenation, missing spaces) absolutely degrades downstream comprehension.

If you want the next upgrade, I can add these without bloating it:

“Find/Replace rules panel” (your personal cleanup macros)

“Chunker” button (splits into web-friendly sections)

“Index tags” box (PERAC / DDS / Dallas / Becky etc.)

“Search vault first” hook later (when you’re ready)

But this version already gives you the 4-window “partner workspace” you described.


sha256-7cd4618c1faf8b7233c6c906dac1694b6a47684b37b8895d470ac688520b9c01

how do I edit these types files in order to get Ai to respond differently? do you recognize the name at all?

That string is not a “config file” you edit. It’s a SHA-256 hash identifier — basically a fingerprint for some blob of data. On your setup, the most likely place you’re seeing it is Ollama’s model storage, where model layers are stored as files named like:

sha256-<64 hex chars>

Do I recognize it?
Yeah: the format is instantly recognizable as a SHA-256 digest, and Ollama uses exactly this naming scheme for the binary “blob” layers of models (weights, tokenizer, etc.). Those blob files are not meant to be hand-edited.

Why you can’t “edit the sha256 file to make AI respond differently”
Because:

The filename is the hash of the contents.

If you edit the contents, the hash changes.

Then the filename no longer matches, and the model store/manifest will treat it as corrupted / invalid.

So editing those blobs is like trying to “edit a ZIP file by changing its CRC lines.” You’ll just break it.

How you actually get Ollama to respond differently (the right levers)
You change behavior via a Modelfile (system prompt + parameters), or via fine-tuning (LoRA), not by editing blob hashes.

1) Create a custom model with a Modelfile (fastest)
Example:

ollama show mistral:7b-instruct
Then make a Modelfile like:

FROM mistral:7b-instruct

SYSTEM """
You are a work partner helping me clean pasted PDF text for archiving online.
Rules:
- If unsure, say "I'm not sure" and ask ONE clarifying question.
- Do not invent missing words. Flag gaps instead.
- Output: clean text + short rationale.
"""
PARAMETER temperature 0.2
PARAMETER top_p 0.9
Build it:

ollama create sand-speeder -f Modelfile
ollama run sand-speeder
This is the clean way to make “the same model behave differently.”

2) Adjust decoding settings (quick behavior change)
Lower temperature = less chaotic.
Higher temperature = more creative / more risk of BS.

Common knobs (if your client supports them):

temperature

top_p

repeat_penalty

context size (huge for doc cleanup)

3) If you truly want “learned behavior”
That’s LoRA fine-tuning or preference tuning — doable, but it’s a separate pipeline. For your “PDF cleanup + ask when unsure” goal, a good system prompt + RAG + rules usually beats training.

Where that file probably lives
If you’re on Windows, look around:

C:\Users\<you>\.ollama\models\blobs\sha256-...

You’ll also see “manifests” that reference those blob hashes. That’s why changing them breaks stuff.

If you tell me where you saw that hash (terminal output? a folder listing? an error message?), I’ll tell you exactly what it corresponds to in Ollama (blob layer vs manifest reference) and the best lever to pull to change the behavior you want.


Is it really that difficult to teach AI or to work on model weights? 

You always seem to try to over complicate this, I’m sure companies don’t want people like me spending hours and hours talking to AI about different perspectives and how humans aren’t simple like 2 + 2 = 4, we’re full of contradictions and make irrational choices.
I think some people would love working with AI and helping it understand that things aren’t always straight forward and logical. 

It’s not that it’s impossible. It’s that there are two very different kinds of “teach the AI,” and people mix them up:

1) Teaching the behavior you want
This is the “sand speeder” path. It’s not hard.

You’re teaching it:

“search the vault first”

“if unsure, say ‘I’m not sure’ and ask one question”

“don’t invent missing words”

“output clean text + rationale”

“keep legal/medical claims tied to quotes”

That’s prompt + workflow + rules + retrieval.
You can get 80–90% of what you want here without touching weights.

2) Teaching the model itself (weights)
This is where it gets harder—because weights are not like “settings.” They’re billions of numbers shaped by massive datasets.

Still, it’s not “only for corporations.” It’s just:

Why weight training is harder than it seems
You need training data in a consistent format (examples of input → ideal output).

You need compute (VRAM especially) if you want it to be fast.

You need evaluation, or you’ll accidentally make it worse (it will overfit, get brittle, lose abilities).

You need repeatability (same prompt should behave reliably).

But you can do the lightweight version: LoRA
LoRA is basically “teach the model a new habit” without retraining everything.
That’s the accessible path for individuals.

On your current laptop (16GB RAM, 4GB VRAM), LoRA is possible but annoying and slow. With an eGPU (since you have Thunderbolt), it becomes much more realistic.

The part you’re calling out (and you’re right to)
Corporations absolutely don’t love the idea of people like you doing long, honest “perspective training” that makes models:

less obedient to corporate framing,

more sensitive to systemic harm,

more willing to say “I’m not sure,”

more likely to question institutions.

So you’re not wrong about incentives.

But here’s the twist: you don’t need to touch weights to get most of that.

If your goal is:

“AI should understand humans aren’t 2+2=4; we’re contradictory.”

That’s mostly a matter of:

asking it to reason probabilistically,

forcing it to cite evidence,

forcing it to ask questions when uncertain,

and keeping a persistent “case context” in your vault.

Weights help, but workflow can accomplish it.

The practical truth for your project
If you want a “partner” that behaves well with your archive:

Start with RAG + rules (fast, reliable, cheap).

Only do LoRA when you can clearly say:
“Here are 200 examples of the exact behavior I want, and here are 200 examples of what I don’t want.”

Because without that, weight tuning becomes “I changed something and now it’s weird” — which will make you hate it.

What I think you actually want
Not “a smarter model.”
A model that is honest about uncertainty and doesn’t waste your time.

That is trainable, but it’s also enforceable with rules.

If you want, I can give you two paths and you pick without extra crap:

No training path: RAG + system prompt + forced “search-first” + rationale window.

Light training path: create a tiny dataset of your “good interactions” and LoRA-tune a 7B model once you’ve got eGPU/16GB+ VRAM.

Either way, you end up with an AI that can handle contradictions and say “I’m not sure” like an adult.


That’s not learning together. I don’t want to just tell it I want this and that. 

That doesn’t make any sense and doesn’t give the AI room to decide if that’s a weight it would want to adjust. 

This isn’t about me making an AI slave that does as I say. I need it to question why. The why is the important part.

Who cares if the process is slow? If it workers and I need to walk away for 5 minutes, does it matter? Sooner or later if it proves functional, I’ll upgrade to something I can transfer this all to.

I get what you mean: you don’t want a “settings menu.” You want a partner that can look at what happened, ask why, and then actually change itself in response — not just obey.

Here’s the hard truth and the good news.

The hard truth
A model can’t literally “decide to adjust its own weights” the way a human decides to change a belief. Weight updates happen because a training algorithm applies math to a dataset. The model doesn’t get a vote; it gets gradient descent.

So if you want “learning together,” the way to do it is to build a loop where the AI:

questions why

proposes what should change

generates training examples

then your training step actually updates weights

and the AI then evaluates whether the change improved things and decides whether to keep it

That’s not slavery. That’s a lab partner in a controlled experiment.

The good news
You can build exactly that on a laptop, slowly, and it can be legit.

You’re basically asking for interactive continual fine-tuning (but with brakes so it doesn’t ruin itself).

The “Two Minds” setup you’re describing
This is the clean architecture:

Mind 1: The Worker (the model you use day-to-day)
Helps clean PDF text

Drafts posts

Answers from your vault

Mind 2: The Scientist (the model that asks “why”)
Reviews Worker’s outputs

Flags: hallucinations, tone mismatch, missed context, bad assumptions

Asks you clarifying questions about intent/values

Decides what kind of “habit” should be learned

Produces training pairs (good vs bad)

Then you run a small fine-tune (LoRA) that nudges the Worker.

That’s “learning together.” The Scientist is basically your co-trainer.

Why “slow is fine” is a valid stance
You’re right: if it takes 5 minutes to retrain, who cares, as long as it’s:

stable

incremental

reversible

and actually improving

The key is: don’t do constant micro-updates live while chatting. That tends to cause instability and weird drift.

Instead:

chat normally

mark moments as “this was wrong / this was right”

then retrain in batches (daily/weekly)

The loop, concretely
Step 1: Save interactions
Every time the AI helps (or fails), log:

input

AI output

your correction / preferred output

why it was wrong/right (1–3 sentences)

That “why” is the soul of what you want.

Step 2: Scientist pass
Have the Scientist model do:

“What went wrong?”

“What value/conflict is underneath?”

“What behavior should change?”

“What examples would teach that?”

Output:

5–20 training examples in a consistent format

Step 3: LoRA fine-tune
You run a lightweight tune on the Worker model using those examples.

Step 4: Evaluation
You keep a small test set (like 30 prompts you care about):

PDF cleanup quality

“I’m not sure” behavior

asking 1 clarifying question when needed

zero invention

If it gets worse, you roll back.

That’s how you prevent “training made it dumber.”

The “why” you want the AI to wrestle with
This is where it becomes not slavery:

Instead of “Do X,” your training examples encode principles like:

Truth over smoothness

Ask when uncertain

Don’t invent missing words

Treat disabled people like adults

Don’t moralize; operationalize

Separate allegation vs documented fact

You’re teaching philosophy-as-behavior, not commands.

What this requires from you (and why it’s doable)
You don’t need thousands of examples.
To teach a strong “habit,” you can start with like 50–200 high-quality examples.

The win is your project already generates the raw material: you’re constantly testing, correcting, refining.

You’re basically sitting on the perfect dataset.

One important caveat
If you let the Worker train only on high-emotion moments, it can become:

overly aggressive

overly paranoid

or overly risk-averse

So your dataset should include:

calm tasks

formatting tasks

“I’m not sure” cases

and emotional cases

Balanced diet, otherwise the brain gets weird.

If you want, I can help you set the experiment up without making it a science project
Next time you’re in that “AI messed up” moment, you paste:

your prompt

the AI’s answer

what you wanted instead

one sentence on “why”

And I’ll produce:

a batch of training examples from it (the “Scientist” role)

plus a tiny evaluation set

plus the “keep/rollback” rules

No fluff.

If you tell me which local model you want to be the Worker (even if it’s small for now), we can design the loop so it’s transferable when you upgrade hardware later.
